[[Block:Dialogue Evaluation]]

MC1. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 23-year-old pregnant woman at 22 weeks gestation presents with burning upon urination. She states it started 1 day ago and has been worsening despite drinking more water and taking cranberry extract. She otherwise feels well and is followed by a doctor for her pregnancy. Her temperature is 97.7F (36.5C), blood pressure is 122/77 mmHg, pulse is 80/min, respirations are 19/min, and oxygen saturation is 98% on room air. Physical exam is notable for an absence of costovertebral angle tenderness and a gravid uterus. Which of the following is the best treatment for this patient? Nitrofurantoin<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi there, I understand you&#x27;re having some trouble. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Hi Doctor. I&#x27;m 22 weeks pregnant, and for the past day, I&#x27;ve had burning when I pee. It&#x27;s really uncomfortable. **Doctor:** I see. And how long has this been going on? **Patient:** Just since yesterday, and it&#x27;s actually getting worse, even though I&#x27;ve been drinking a lot of water and taking cranberry extract. **Doctor:** I understand. Other than the burning, how are you feeling? **Patient:** I feel fine otherwise. My pregnancy is being followed regularly by my OB. **Doctor:** Okay, let&#x27;s take a look. I&#x27;m going to check your vitals and do a quick exam. *[Doctor takes vitals]* Your temperature is 97.7, blood pressure is 122 over 77, pulse is 80, respirations are 19, and your oxygen is 98%. Everything looks good there. I&#x27;m also checking for any tenderness in your back near your kidneys... that&#x27;s good, no tenderness. And I can feel your uterus is growing as expected. Given your symptoms and the examination, the best treatment for this is Nitrofurantoin.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC2. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 3-month-old baby died suddenly at night while asleep. His mother noticed that he had died only after she awoke in the morning. No cause of death was determined based on the autopsy. Which of the following precautions could have prevented the death of the baby? Placing the infant in a supine position on a firm mattress while sleeping<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Johnson. I understand you brought your 3-month-old son in for a check-up? **Mrs. Johnson:** Good morning, Doctor. Actually, it&#x27;s... it&#x27;s about what happened. He... he passed away. **Doctor:** Oh, I am so deeply sorry to hear that, Mrs. Johnson. I can&#x27;t imagine how difficult this must be. Can you tell me what happened? **Mrs. Johnson:** He seemed fine when I put him to bed last night. But when I woke up this morning, he... he wasn&#x27;t breathing. **Doctor:** I understand. Was there anything unusual about his health leading up to this? Any fever, cough, or difficulty feeding? **Mrs. Johnson:** No, nothing at all. He was perfectly healthy. **Doctor:** And you mentioned an autopsy was performed? **Mrs. Johnson:** Yes, it was. But they couldn&#x27;t find anything. No cause of death. It&#x27;s just... unexplained. **Doctor:** I see. Unfortunately, in some cases, despite thorough investigation, the exact cause remains undetermined. While I can&#x27;t bring your son back, it is important to consider preventative measures for future infants. One of the most important things we can do to reduce the risk of sudden infant death is **Placing the infant in a supine position on a firm mattress while sleeping**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC3. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A mother brings her 3-week-old infant to the pediatrician&#x27;s office because she is concerned about his feeding habits. He was born without complications and has not had any medical problems up until this time. However, for the past 4 days, he has been fussy, is regurgitating all of his feeds, and his vomit is yellow in color. On physical exam, the child&#x27;s abdomen is minimally distended but no other abnormalities are appreciated. Which of the following embryologic errors could account for this presentation? Abnormal migration of ventral pancreatic bud<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi Mrs. Smith, thanks for bringing little Timmy in. What seems to be the trouble? **Mrs. Smith:** Well, Doctor, I&#x27;m worried about his feeding. He&#x27;s three weeks old, and for the past four days he&#x27;s been so fussy, and he&#x27;s regurgitating everything he eats. **Doctor:** Okay, and was his birth normal, any complications? **Mrs. Smith:** No, everything was fine at birth. He hasn&#x27;t had any medical problems until now. **Doctor:** I see. And what does the vomit look like? **Mrs. Smith:** It&#x27;s yellow, Doctor. **Doctor:** Alright. Let&#x27;s take a look at him. (Examines Timmy) His abdomen is a little distended, but otherwise, he seems okay on the exam. Given his age, symptoms, and what I&#x27;m seeing here, we need to consider some possibilities. One potential cause, based on what we know about development, could be **Abnormal migration of ventral pancreatic bud**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC4. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A pulmonary autopsy specimen from a 58-year-old woman who died of acute hypoxic respiratory failure was examined. She had recently undergone surgery for a fractured femur 3 months ago. Initial hospital course was uncomplicated, and she was discharged to a rehab facility in good health. Shortly after discharge home from rehab, she developed sudden shortness of breath and had cardiac arrest. Resuscitation was unsuccessful. On histological examination of lung tissue, fibrous connective tissue around the lumen of the pulmonary artery is observed. Which of the following is the most likely pathogenesis for the present findings? Thromboembolism<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, I&#x27;m reviewing the autopsy results for the 58-year-old woman who passed away from acute respiratory failure. It&#x27;s a difficult case. **Doctor:** As I understand it, she had a fractured femur about three months ago and underwent surgery. **Patient&#x27;s Relative:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. The surgery went well, and she was discharged to rehab. Everything seemed to be going fine. **Doctor:** And she was discharged home from rehab in good condition, correct? **Patient&#x27;s Relative:** Yes, but shortly after she came home, she suddenly developed shortness of breath and then went into cardiac arrest. They couldn&#x27;t revive her. **Doctor:** I&#x27;m so sorry for your loss. The autopsy shows some interesting findings in her lungs. Histologically, we observed fibrous connective tissue around the lumen of her pulmonary artery. Considering her recent surgery and subsequent events, the most likely pathogenesis for these findings is **Thromboembolism**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC5. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 20-year-old woman presents with menorrhagia for the past several years. She says that her menses have always been heavy, and she has experienced easy bruising for as long as she can remember. Family history is significant for her mother, who had similar problems with bruising easily. The patient&#x27;s vital signs include: heart rate 98/min, respiratory rate 14/min, temperature 36.1C (96.9F), and blood pressure 110/87 mm Hg. Physical examination is unremarkable. Laboratory tests show the following: platelet count 200,000/mm3, PT 12 seconds, and PTT 43 seconds. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patients symptoms? Von Willebrand disease<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, please have a seat. So, you&#x27;re here today because you&#x27;ve been experiencing some heavy periods, right? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. For the past several years, my periods have been really heavy, like, heavier than other people&#x27;s. I always thought it was normal, because they&#x27;ve always been this way. **Doctor:** And you mentioned you&#x27;ve also been bruising easily? **Patient:** Yes, I bruise really easily. I don&#x27;t even remember hitting anything sometimes, and I&#x27;ll have a huge bruise. It&#x27;s been like that for as long as I can remember. **Doctor:** Okay. And does anyone else in your family have similar issues? **Patient:** Actually, my mother has always had problems with bruising easily too. **Doctor:** Alright. Let&#x27;s take some vitals. Your heart rate is 98, respiratory rate is 14, temperature is 96.9, and your blood pressure is 110 over 87. Everything seems normal there. I&#x27;ll just do a quick physical exam... Everything seems unremarkable. We&#x27;ve also done some blood tests, and the results are back. Your platelet count is 200,000, your PT is 12 seconds, and your PTT is 43 seconds. Based on your symptoms, family history, and these lab results, the most likely diagnosis is **Von Willebrand disease**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC6. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 40-year-old zookeeper presents to the emergency department complaining of severe abdominal pain that radiates to her back, and nausea. The pain started 2 days ago and slowly increased until she could not tolerate it any longer. Past medical history is significant for hypertension and hypothyroidism. Additionally, she reports that she was recently stung by one of the zoos smaller scorpions, but did not seek medical treatment. She takes aspirin, levothyroxine, oral contraceptive pills, and a multivitamin daily. Family history is noncontributory. Today, her blood pressure is 108/58 mm Hg, heart rate is 99/min, respiratory rate is 21/min, and temperature is 37.0C (98.6F). On physical exam, she is a well-developed, obese female that looks unwell. Her heart has a regular rate and rhythm. Radial pulses are weak but symmetric. Her lungs are clear to auscultation bilaterally. Her lateral left ankle is swollen, erythematous, and painful to palpate. An abdominal CT is consistent with acute pancreatitis. Which of the following is the most likely etiology for this patients disease? Scorpion sting<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, I&#x27;m Dr. Ramirez. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Doctor, I&#x27;ve been having terrible abdominal pain for the past two days. It&#x27;s radiating to my back, and I&#x27;m feeling really nauseous. It started gradually, but it&#x27;s gotten so bad I can&#x27;t take it anymore. **Doctor:** I see. Can you tell me a little about your medical history? Do you have any existing conditions? **Patient:** Yes, I have hypertension and hypothyroidism. I&#x27;m also a zookeeper. **Doctor:** Okay. And are you taking any medications? **Patient:** I take aspirin, levothyroxine, oral contraceptive pills, and a multivitamin, all daily. **Doctor:** Alright. And is there anything else I should know? Any recent injuries or incidents? **Patient:** Well, about a week ago, I was stung by one of the smaller scorpions at the zoo. I didn&#x27;t think much of it at the time, so I didn&#x27;t seek any medical treatment. **Doctor:** Okay. And what about your family history? Any significant illnesses? **Patient:** No, nothing significant that I know of. **Doctor:** Let me take some vitals and examine you. Your blood pressure is 108/58, your heart rate is 99, respiratory rate is 21, and your temperature is 37 degrees Celsius. You look unwell, and I can see that you are obese. Your heart sounds normal, although your radial pulses are a bit weak. Your lungs sound clear. I also notice your left ankle is swollen, red, and painful to the touch. **Doctor:** Ive ordered an abdominal CT scan, and the results are back. They show that you have acute pancreatitis. Considering everything, your symptoms, the exam findings, and your history, the most likely etiology for this patients disease is: Scorpion sting

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC7. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 25-year-old primigravida presents to her physician for a routine prenatal visit. She is at 34 weeks gestation, as confirmed by an ultrasound examination. She has no complaints, but notes that the new shoes she bought 2 weeks ago do not fit anymore. The course of her pregnancy has been uneventful and she has been compliant with the recommended prenatal care. Her medical history is unremarkable. She has a 15-pound weight gain since the last visit 3 weeks ago. Her vital signs are as follows: blood pressure, 148/90 mm Hg; heart rate, 88/min; respiratory rate, 16/min; and temperature, 36.6 (97.9). The blood pressure on repeat assessment 4 hours later is 151/90 mm Hg. The fetal heart rate is 151/min. The physical examination is significant for 2+ pitting edema of the lower extremity. Which of the following tests o should confirm the probable condition of this patient? 24-hour urine protein<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi there! Welcome in. How are you feeling today? **Patient:** I&#x27;m feeling alright, doctor. Just here for my regular check-up. I&#x27;m 34 weeks along now, right? **Doctor:** That&#x27;s right, 34 weeks. We confirmed that with the ultrasound. And how has everything been going? **Patient:** Pretty smoothly, actually. No real complaints. I did notice something a little weird though. **Doctor:** What&#x27;s that? **Patient:** My new shoes, the ones I bought a couple of weeks ago, they don&#x27;t fit anymore! **Doctor:** Okay. And has anything else changed since your last visit? Which was about three weeks ago, if I remember correctly. **Patient:** Not really. I&#x27;ve been keeping up with everything you recommended. Oh, I did notice I&#x27;ve gained a bit of weight, maybe a little more than usual. **Doctor:** Let&#x27;s see. You&#x27;ve gained 15 pounds since your last appointment. We&#x27;ll check your vitals now. Your blood pressure is a little elevated at 148 over 90, heart rate is 88, breathing is 16, and temperature is 97.9. Let me check your blood pressure again in a few hours to be sure. Okay, after four hours, your blood pressure is still high at 151 over 90. The baby&#x27;s heart rate is good, 151. And I&#x27;m also noticing you have some swelling in your legs, 2+ pitting edema. **Patient:** Swelling? Is that something to be concerned about? **Doctor:** Given your blood pressure and the swelling, I want to investigate a little further. To confirm what&#x27;s going on, I&#x27;d like you to do a **24-hour urine protein**.

1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.

MC8. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 3900-g (8.6-lb) male infant is delivered at 39 weeks&#x27; gestation via spontaneous vaginal delivery. Pregnancy and delivery were uncomplicated but a prenatal ultrasound at 20 weeks showed a defect in the pleuroperitoneal membrane. Further evaluation of this patient is most likely to show which of the following findings? Gastric fundus in the thorax<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi there, welcome. So, this is your baby boy, delivered vaginally at 39 weeks, weighing in at 8 pounds 6 ounces. Congratulations! Everything went smoothly during the pregnancy and delivery, I understand? **Patient:** Yes, everything was pretty straightforward. **Doctor:** Good. Now, during your 20-week ultrasound, they noted a potential issue with the pleuroperitoneal membrane. That&#x27;s the membrane that separates the chest and abdominal cavities. **Patient:** Yes, I remember them mentioning something like that. What does that mean for him? **Doctor:** Well, based on that finding, and given his otherwise healthy delivery, further evaluation is most likely to show... **Gastric fundus in the thorax**.

MC9. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 62-year-old woman presents for a regular check-up. She complains of lightheadedness and palpitations which occur episodically. Past medical history is significant for a myocardial infarction 6 months ago and NYHA class II chronic heart failure. She also was diagnosed with grade I arterial hypertension 4 years ago. Current medications are aspirin 81 mg, atorvastatin 10 mg, enalapril 10 mg, and metoprolol 200 mg daily. Her vital signs are a blood pressure of 135/90 mm Hg, a heart rate of 125/min, a respiratory rate of 14/min, and a temperature of 36.5C (97.7F). Cardiopulmonary examination is significant for irregular heart rhythm and decreased S1 intensity. ECG is obtained and is shown in the picture (see image). Echocardiography shows a left ventricular ejection fraction of 39%. Which of the following drugs is the best choice for rate control in this patient? Digoxin<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Smith. How are you feeling today? **Mrs. Smith:** Well, Doctor, Ive been experiencing some lightheadedness and palpitations off and on. It&#x27;s a bit concerning. **Doctor:** I understand. And youre here for your regular check-up, right? Lets take a look at your history. You had a myocardial infarction about six months ago, correct? **Mrs. Smith:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. And I&#x27;ve been told I have chronic heart failure, NYHA class II. **Doctor:** And you were diagnosed with grade I arterial hypertension a few years back, about four years ago? **Mrs. Smith:** Yes, that&#x27;s correct. **Doctor:** Okay. And what medications are you currently taking? **Mrs. Smith:** I&#x27;m on aspirin 81 mg, atorvastatin 10 mg, enalapril 10 mg, and metoprolol 200 mg daily. **Doctor:** Alright. Let&#x27;s check your vitals. Your blood pressure is 135/90 mm Hg, heart rate is 125, respiratory rate is 14, and temperature is 36.5C. Okay, let&#x27;s listen to your heart and lungs. I do hear an irregular heart rhythm and the first heart sound, S1, is a bit diminished. Given your symptoms and history, I&#x27;m going to get an ECG to see what&#x27;s going on with your heart rhythm. **Mrs. Smith:** Okay, Doctor. **Doctor:** (After reviewing the ECG and echocardiogram results) Okay, Mrs. Smith, the ECG shows what&#x27;s going on with the irregular rhythm, and your echocardiogram shows your left ventricular ejection fraction is 39%. Taking everything into consideration  your history of heart failure, your palpitations, lightheadedness, and the ECG results  the best choice for rate control in your case is **Digoxin**. We&#x27;ll discuss the dosage and potential side effects.

1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.

MC10. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 35-year-old male presents to his primary care physician with complaints of seasonal allergies. He has been using intranasal vasoconstrictors several times per day for several weeks. What is a likely sequela of the chronic use of topical nasal decongestants? Persistent congestion<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi there, thanks for coming in. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Well, I&#x27;ve been having a lot of trouble with my seasonal allergies lately. They&#x27;re really acting up. **Doctor:** I see. And how long has this been going on? **Patient:** A few weeks now, maybe a month. I&#x27;ve been using nasal spray to try and clear things up. **Doctor:** Nasal spray, okay. And how often are you using it? **Patient:** Pretty frequently, actually. Several times a day, every day. It&#x27;s the only thing that seems to give me any relief. **Doctor:** I understand. So, you&#x27;ve been using an intranasal vasoconstrictor, a topical nasal decongestant, multiple times daily for several weeks. Based on this, a likely sequela is **Persistent congestion**.

MC11. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 46-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 2-week history of diplopia and ocular pain when reading the newspaper. She also has a 3-month history of amenorrhea, hot flashes, and increased sweating. She reports that she has been overweight all her adult life and is happy to have lost 6.8-kg (15-lb) of weight in the past 2 months. Her pulse is 110/min, and blood pressure is 148/98 mm Hg. Physical examination shows moist palms and a nontender thyroid gland that is enlarged to two times its normal size. Ophthalmologic examination shows prominence of the globes of the eyes, bilateral lid retraction, conjunctival injection, and an inability to converge the eyes. There is no pain on movement of the extraocular muscles. Visual acuity is 20/20 bilaterally. Neurologic examination shows a fine resting tremor of the hands. Deep tendon reflexes are 3+ with a shortened relaxation phase. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient&#x27;s ocular complaints? Glycosaminoglycan accumulation in the orbit<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, please have a seat. So, what brings you in today? **Patient:** Well, for the past two weeks, I&#x27;ve been experiencing double vision, especially when I&#x27;m trying to read. It&#x27;s accompanied by some pain in my eyes. **Doctor:** I see. And anything else going on? **Patient:** Yes, actually. For the last three months, I haven&#x27;t had my period, and I&#x27;ve been getting these awful hot flashes and sweating a lot. Also, I know it sounds strange, but I&#x27;ve actually been happy about losing about 15 pounds over the last two months. I&#x27;ve struggled with my weight for years. **Doctor:** Okay. Let me take your vitals. *[Takes pulse and blood pressure]* Your pulse is a bit high at 110, and your blood pressure is elevated at 148/98. Let&#x27;s do a physical exam. *[Examines patient]* Your palms are quite moist, and your thyroid gland is enlarged, about twice its normal size, but it doesn&#x27;t seem tender. Now, let&#x27;s take a look at your eyes. *[Performs ophthalmologic examination]* I see some prominence of your eyeballs, lid retraction in both eyes, and some redness in the conjunctiva. It also looks like you&#x27;re having trouble converging your eyes. Does it hurt when you move your eyes around? **Patient:** No, there&#x27;s no pain with eye movement. **Doctor:** Okay. Your visual acuity is 20/20 in both eyes, which is good. *[Performs neurologic examination]* I notice a slight tremor in your hands. And your reflexes are quite brisk, 3+, with a quick relaxation. **Patient:** So, what do you think is causing my eye problems, Doctor? **Doctor:** Based on everything you&#x27;ve told me and what I&#x27;ve observed during the examination, the most likely cause of your ocular complaints is **Glycosaminoglycan accumulation in the orbit**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions

MC12. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 1-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with weakness and a change in his behavior. His parents state that they first noticed the change in his behavior this morning and it has been getting worse. They noticed the patient was initially weak in his upper body and arms, but now he wont move his legs with as much strength or vigor as he used to. Physical exam is notable for bilateral ptosis with a sluggish pupillary response, a very weak sucking and gag reflex, and shallow respirations. The patient is currently drooling and his diaper is dry. The parents state he has not had a bowel movement in over 1 day. Which of the following is the pathophysiology of this patients condition? Blockade of presynaptic acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, I understand you&#x27;re here today because you&#x27;re concerned about your son. Can you tell me what&#x27;s been happening? **Parents:** Yes, doctor. We noticed a change in his behavior this morning, and it seems to be getting worse. He&#x27;s just not acting like himself. **Doctor:** Can you describe the change you&#x27;ve observed? **Parents:** Well, initially, we noticed he was weak, especially in his upper body and arms. Now, he&#x27;s not moving his legs as much, and he doesn&#x27;t seem to have the same strength he usually does. **Doctor:** Okay. And when did you first notice the weakness? **Parents:** Just this morning. It&#x27;s been progressing throughout the day. **Doctor:** I see. Let&#x27;s take a look at him. (Doctor performs a physical exam) I&#x27;m noticing he has some droopiness in his eyelids, what we call ptosis, and his pupils are responding slowly to light. His sucking and gag reflex are also very weak, and his breathing seems shallow. I also see he&#x27;s drooling, and you mentioned his diaper is dry? **Parents:** Yes, he hasn&#x27;t had a wet diaper in a while, and he hasn&#x27;t had a bowel movement since yesterday. **Doctor:** Alright. Based on what you&#x27;ve told me and my examination, it looks like the pathophysiology of his condition is **Blockade of presynaptic acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction**.

MC13. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 9-month-old female is brought to the emergency department after experiencing a seizure. She was born at home and was normal at birth according to her parents. Since then, they have noticed that she does not appear to be achieving developmental milestones as quickly as her siblings, and often appears lethargic. Physical exam reveals microcephaly, very light pigmentation (as compared to her family), and a &quot;musty&quot; body odor. The varied manifestations of this disease can most likely be attributed to which of the following genetic principles? Pleiotropy<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, I understand you brought your daughter in today. What seems to be the problem? **Parents:** Doctor, she had a seizure earlier. We were so scared. **Doctor:** I understand. How old is she? **Parents:** Shes 9 months old. **Doctor:** Okay. And was she born at the hospital? **Parents:** No, she was born at home. But she seemed perfectly normal at birth, according to us. **Doctor:** Alright. Have you noticed anything else that&#x27;s been concerning you besides the seizure? **Parents:** Well, yes. Weve noticed she&#x27;s not really developing as quickly as our other children did at this age. She seems a bitlethargic most of the time. **Doctor:** I see. I&#x27;m going to examine her now. *[Doctor examines the baby]* Im noticing she has a slightly smaller head circumference than expected, what we call microcephaly. <strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A researcher is studying the properties of an enzyme that adds phosphate groups to glucose. She discovers that the enzyme is present in most body tissues and is located in the cytoplasm of the cells expressing the enzyme. She decides to mix this enzyme under subphysiologic conditions with varying levels of glucose in order to determine the kinetic properties of the enzyme. Specifically, she adds increasing levels of glucose at a saturating concentration of phosphate and sees that the rate at which glucose becomes phosphorylated gets faster at higher levels of glucose. She observes that this rate approaches a maximum speed and calls this speed Y. She then determines the concentration of glucose that is needed to make the enzyme function at half the speed Y and calls this concentration X. Which of the following is most likely true about the properties of this enzyme? Low X and low Y<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hello. So, you&#x27;re here today because you&#x27;re interested in the properties of an enzyme, right? Let&#x27;s talk about it. **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. I&#x27;ve been researching this enzyme that adds phosphate groups to glucose. I&#x27;ve found it&#x27;s pretty much everywhere in the body, in most tissues, and it&#x27;s always inside the cells, specifically in the cytoplasm. **Doctor:** Okay, and what have you learned about how it works? **Patient:** Well, I&#x27;ve been testing it under conditions that aren&#x27;t quite the same as inside the body, to see how it reacts to different amounts of glucose. I&#x27;ve made sure there&#x27;s plenty of phosphate available, and then I&#x27;ve gradually increased the amount of glucose. **Doctor:** And what happened as you increased the glucose? **Patient:** As I added more and more glucose, the rate at which it got phosphorylated, that is, the rate at which the enzyme added a phosphate group to the glucose, got faster and faster. **Doctor:** Did it keep getting faster indefinitely? **Patient:** No, eventually it reached a maximum speed. I&#x27;ve called that maximum speed Y. **Doctor:** I see. And what else did you discover? **Patient:** I also figured out the concentration of glucose needed to make the enzyme work at half of that maximum speed, Y. I&#x27;ve called that concentration X. **Doctor:** So, based on your findings, what can you say about the properties of this enzyme? What is the relationship between X and Y? **Patient:** Low X and low Y.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A new screening test utilizing a telemedicine approach to diagnosing diabetic retinopathy has been implemented in a diabetes clinic. An ophthalmologists exam was also performed on all patients as the gold standard for diagnosis. In a pilot study of 500 patients, the screening test detected the presence of diabetic retinopathy in 250 patients. Ophthalmologist exam confirmed a diagnosis of diabetic retinopathy in 200 patients who tested positive in the screening test, as well as 10 patients who tested negative in the screening test. What is the sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, and negative predictive value of the screening test? Sensitivity = 95%, Specificity = 83%, PPV = 80%, NPV = 96%<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Davis. Welcome back. I understand you&#x27;re here today for your regular diabetes check-up. We&#x27;re also implementing a new screening test for diabetic retinopathy in the clinic, and you were part of the pilot study. **Mrs. Davis:** Yes, I remember. It was all done remotely, right? **Doctor:** That&#x27;s correct. It&#x27;s a telemedicine approach. So, in the pilot study with 500 patients, including yourself, the screening test flagged 250 people as potentially having diabetic retinopathy. We then had an ophthalmologist perform a thorough eye exam on everyone, which is our gold standard for diagnosis. **Mrs. Davis:** Okay, and what did the ophthalmologist find? **Doctor:** Well, of those 250 people who tested positive with the screening test, the ophthalmologist confirmed that 200 actually had diabetic retinopathy. Interestingly, the ophthalmologist also found that 10 patients who tested negative with the screening test actually *did* have diabetic retinopathy. **Mrs. Davis:** So, it wasn&#x27;t perfect then? Some people got misdiagnosed? **Doctor:** That&#x27;s right. No test is perfect. To understand how well the screening test performs, we calculate things like sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, and negative predictive value. In this case, the results are: Sensitivity = 95%, Specificity = 83%, PPV = 80%, NPV = 96%.

Her first child was born at term by vaginal delivery. She has no history of serious illness. She does not drink alcohol or smoke cigarettes. Current medications include vitamin supplements. Her temperature is 37.2C (98.9F), pulse is 70/min, respirations are 18/min, and blood pressure is 128/82 mm Hg. Speculum examination demonstrates clear fluid in the cervical canal. The fetal heart rate is reactive at 160/min with no decelerations. Tocometry shows uterine contractions. Nitrazine testing is positive. She is started on indomethacin. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? Administer betamethasone and ampicillin<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, I&#x27;m Dr. Ramirez. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Well, I&#x27;m 32 weeks pregnant, and I&#x27;ve suddenly started leaking clear fluid vaginally. I&#x27;m a bit worried. **Doctor:** I understand. This is your second pregnancy, correct? **Patient:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. My first was a normal vaginal delivery, full-term. **Doctor:** And how has this pregnancy been so far? Any complications? **Patient:** No, it&#x27;s been pretty uneventful, thankfully. I&#x27;ve been taking my vitamins, and I don&#x27;t drink or smoke. I&#x27;ve never had any serious illnesses either. **Doctor:** Okay. Let me take your vitals. (Takes vitals). Your temperature is 98.9, pulse is 70, respirations are 18, and blood pressure is 128 over 82. I&#x27;m going to do a quick exam now. (Performs speculum exam). I see clear fluid in the cervical canal. We&#x27;ll also check the fetal heart rate and monitor for contractions. (Checks fetal heart rate and performs tocometry). The fetal heart rate is reactive at 160, which is good, and we see some uterine contractions. The Nitrazine test is positive as well. **Patient:** What does that mean? **Doctor:** The Nitrazine test result confirms that the fluid is amniotic fluid. We&#x27;ve started you on indomethacin to help manage the contractions. Now, considering everything, the next step in management is to **Administer betamethasone and ampicillin**.

His oxygen levels are dangerously low  only 64%. We performed an autopsy, and the examination of his spinal cord showed destruction of the anterior horn cells. Based on these findings, a neurological examination would have most likely shown **Hyporeflexia**.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A woman with coronary artery disease is starting to go for a walk. As she begins, her heart rate accelerates from a resting pulse of 60 bpm until it reaches a rate of 120 bpm, at which point she begins to feel a tightening in her chest. She stops walking to rest and the tightening resolves. This has been happening to her consistently for the last 6 months. Which of the following is a true statement? Increasing the heart rate decreases the relative amount of time spent during diastole<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, please have a seat. For example, when my heart rate goes from resting to about 120 beats per minute, I get this squeezing feeling in my chest. **Doctor:** And what do you do when that happens? **Patient:** I stop and rest, and then it goes away. **Doctor:** Okay. And you said this has been happening for about six months? Do you have any other medical conditions? **Patient:** Yes, I have coronary artery disease. **Doctor:** Alright. I&#x27;m going to listen to your heart and lungs. *[Doctor performs examination]* Everything sounds normal at rest. Based on what you&#x27;ve told me, the chest pain you&#x27;re experiencing when your heart rate increases is very likely angina. **Patient:** Angina? What causes that? **Doctor:** It&#x27;s essentially a sign that your heart muscle isn&#x27;t getting enough blood when it needs it most, like when you&#x27;re exercising. In this case, the statement that best describes why you are experiencing this symptom is: Increasing the heart rate decreases the relative amount of time spent during diastole. **Patient:** So, what can we do about it? **Doctor:** We have several options. We can start with medication to help manage the angina. We might also consider further testing, like a stress test, to get a better idea of the severity of your coronary artery disease. We can then discuss the best treatment plan for you, which may involve lifestyle changes, medication, or even a procedure to improve blood flow to your heart.

MC100. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
You are reviewing raw data from a research study performed at your medical center examining the effectiveness of a novel AIDS screening examination. The study enrolled 250 patients with confirmed AIDS, and 240 of these patients demonstrated a positive screening examination. The control arm of the study enrolled 250 patients who do not have AIDS, and only 5 of these patients tested positive on the novel screening examination. What is the NPV of this novel test? 245 / (245 + 10)<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. Jones. Thanks for coming in today. I&#x27;ve been reviewing some data from a research study we conducted here, and I wanted to discuss the results with you. **Patient:** Okay, Doctor. What&#x27;s it about? **Doctor:** It was a study to evaluate a new screening test for AIDS. We had two groups: one with 250 patients who we knew had AIDS, and another control group of 250 patients who did not have AIDS. **Patient:** And what did the test show? **Doctor:** In the group that had AIDS, the screening test came back positive for 240 of those individuals. In the group that didn&#x27;t have AIDS, only 5 people tested positive. **Patient:** So, what does that tell you about the test? **Doctor:** Well, we can calculate what&#x27;s called the Negative Predictive Value, or NPV, of the test. This tells us the probability that someone who tests negative actually *doesn&#x27;t* have the disease. In this case, to calculate the NPV, we need to consider the number of true negatives and false negatives. From our data, we can determine that the NPV is: 245 / (245 + 10).

MC102. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A case-control study is conducted to investigate the association between the use of phenytoin during pregnancy in women with epilepsy and the risk for congenital malformations. The odds ratio of congenital malformations in newborns born to women who were undergoing treatment with phenytoin is 1.74 (P = 0.02) compared to newborns of women who were not treated with phenytoin. Which of the following 95% confidence intervals is most likely reported for this association? 1.34 to 2.36<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi Sarah, thanks for coming in today. **Sarah:** Hi Doctor. **Doctor:** So, as we discussed, we&#x27;re looking at the potential effects of phenytoin, which you&#x27;re taking for your epilepsy, on pregnancies. We&#x27;re doing a study comparing women with epilepsy who took phenytoin during pregnancy to those who didn&#x27;t, and looking at the rates of congenital malformations in their newborns. **Sarah:** Okay, that makes sense. I&#x27;m a little worried about that. **Doctor:** I understand. Well, we&#x27;ve found that the odds ratio for congenital malformations in newborns whose mothers were on phenytoin is 1.74. The p-value is 0.02, which is statistically significant. **Sarah:** What does that actually mean, 1.74? **Doctor:** It means that newborns of women who were treated with phenytoin have a 1.74 times higher odds of having congenital malformations compared to newborns of women who weren&#x27;t treated with phenytoin. Now, we also need to express this finding with a confidence interval to provide a range of plausible values. The most likely 95% confidence interval reported for this association would be **1.34 to 2.36**. This means we are 95% confident that the true odds ratio lies within that range.

The patient has an anion gap metabolic acidosis with decreased total body potassium<br><br>

A 61-year-old woman presents to her physician with a persistent cough. She has been unable to control her cough and also is finding it increasingly difficult to breathe. The cough has been persistent for about 2 months now, but 2 weeks ago she started noticing streaks of blood in the sputum regularly after coughing. Over the course of 4 months, she has also observed an unusual loss of 10 kg (22 lb) in her weight. She has an unchanged appetite and remains fairly active, which makes her suspicious as to the cause of her weight loss. Another troublesome concern for her is that on a couple occasions over the past few weeks, she has observed herself drenched in sweat when she wakes up in the morning. Other than having a 35 pack-year smoking history, her medical history is insignificant. She is sent for a chest X-ray which shows a central nodule of about 13 mm located in the hilar region. Which of the following would be the next best step in the management of this patient? Mediastinoscopy<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mrs. Smith. Please, have a seat. So, I understand you&#x27;ve been having some trouble with a cough? **Mrs. Smith:** Yes, Doctor. It&#x27;s been persistent for about two months now, and it&#x27;s really getting to me. I can&#x27;t seem to shake it. **Doctor:** And what else have you noticed? **Mrs. Smith:** Well, it&#x27;s been getting harder to breathe, and about two weeks ago, I started seeing streaks of blood in my sputum after I cough. That really worried me. **Doctor:** I understand. Anything else you&#x27;ve noticed recently? **Mrs. Smith:** Yes, I&#x27;ve lost about 10 kilograms, which is around 22 pounds, over the past four months. I haven&#x27;t changed my diet, and I&#x27;m still fairly active, so I don&#x27;t understand it. Also, I&#x27;ve been waking up drenched in sweat a couple of times a week. **Doctor:** Okay. And can you tell me about your medical history? Do you smoke? **Mrs. Smith:** I do, yes. I have a 35 pack-year smoking history. Other than that, I don&#x27;t have any significant medical problems. **Doctor:** Alright. We took a chest X-ray, and it shows a small nodule, about 13 millimeters, located in the hilar region. Given everything you&#x27;ve told me and what we&#x27;ve seen on the X-ray, the next best step in determining what&#x27;s going on is a **Mediastinoscopy**.

A 2-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department by his parents after they found him to be lethargic and febrile. His current symptoms started 1 week ago and initially consisted of a sore throat and a runny nose. He subsequently developed a fever and productive cough that has become worse over time. Notably, this patient has previously presented with pneumonia and gastroenteritis 8 times since he was born. On presentation, the patient&#x27;s temperature is 103F (39.4C), blood pressure is 90/50 mmHg, pulse is 152/min, and respirations are 38/min. Based on clinical suspicion, an antibody panel is obtained and the results show low levels of IgG and IgA relative to the level of IgM. The expression of which of the following genes is most likely abnormal in this patient? CD40L<br><br>

It started about a week ago with just a sore throat and runny nose, but then he developed a fever and a cough that&#x27;s just gotten worse. He&#x27;s only two years old, and we&#x27;re very concerned. **Doctor:** I understand. Has he had any similar issues before? **Parents:** Yes, actually. He&#x27;s had pneumonia and gastroenteritis quite a few times, about eight times since he was born. That&#x27;s why we&#x27;re extra worried. **Doctor:** Okay. Let me take a look at him. *[Doctor examines the child]* His temperature is 103F, blood pressure is 90 over 50, pulse is 152, and his respirations are 38. We&#x27;re going to run some tests. Based on what you&#x27;ve told me about his history and what I&#x27;m seeing now, I suspect there might be an issue with his immune system. **Doctor:** *[After reviewing the test results]* Okay, the antibody panel came back. His IgG and IgA levels are low, but his IgM is normal. Considering the recurrent infections and these antibody levels, it points to a specific genetic problem. The expression of which of the following genes is most likely abnormal in this patient? **CD40L**

MC123. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 71-year-old man comes to the emergency department because of a 2-month history of severe muscle cramps and back pain. He says that he is homeless and has not visited a physician in the past 20 years. He is 183 cm (6 ft 0 in) tall and weighs 62 kg (137 lb); BMI is 18.5 kg/m2. His blood pressure is 154/88 mm Hg. Physical examination shows pallor, multiple cutaneous excoriations, and decreased sensation over the lower extremities. Serum studies show: Calcium 7.2 mg/dL Phosphorus 5.1 mg/dL Glucose 221 mg/dL Creatinine 4.5 mg/dL An x-ray of the spine shows alternating sclerotic and radiolucent bands in the lumbar and thoracic vertebral bodies. Which of the following is the most likely explanation for these findings?&quot; Secondary hyperparathyroidism<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, sir. I understand you&#x27;ve been having some trouble. Can you tell me what brought you in today? **Patient:** Well, doc, for the past couple of months, I&#x27;ve been getting terrible muscle cramps, and my back has been killing me. **Doctor:** I see. And how long have you been experiencing these symptoms? **Patient:** About two months now. **Doctor:** Okay. Have you seen a doctor recently? **Patient:** Not in about 20 years, Doc. I&#x27;m homeless, you see. **Doctor:** I understand. Let&#x27;s get some basic information. How tall are you and what&#x27;s your weight? **Patient:** I&#x27;m about 6 feet tall and weigh around 137 pounds. **Doctor:** Okay. Your blood pressure is a bit elevated, 154 over 88. I&#x27;ve also noticed you&#x27;re a bit pale and have some skin abrasions. And your sensation is decreased in your legs. **Patient:** Yeah, I&#x27;ve noticed that too. **Doctor:** We ran some blood tests. Your calcium is quite low, 7.2 mg/dL, and your phosphorus is a bit high at 5.1 mg/dL. Your blood sugar is also elevated at 221 mg/dL, and your creatinine is 4.5 mg/dL, which is concerning. **Patient:** What does that mean, Doc? **Doctor:** It means we need to investigate further. We also took an X-ray of your spine. It shows some unusual changes  alternating dark and light bands in your back bones. Taking everything into account, all your symptoms, the lab results, and the X-ray findings, the most likely explanation is **Secondary hyperparathyroidism**.

A 23-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician for knee pain. The patient states it started yesterday and has been steadily worsening. She recently joined a volleyball team to try to get into shape as she was informed that weight loss would be beneficial for her at her last physical exam. She states that she has been repetitively pivoting and twisting on her knee while playing volleyball. The patient has a past medical history of polycystic ovarian syndrome and is currently taking oral contraceptive pills. Her temperature is 98.5F (36.9C), blood pressure is 137/88 mmHg, pulse is 90/min, respirations are 12/min, and oxygen saturation is 98% on room air. Physical exam reveals an obese woman with facial hair. Physical exam is notable for tenderness that is mediated with palpation over the medial aspect of the tibia just inferior to the patella. Her BMI is 37 kg/m^2. The rest of the exam of the lower extremity is not remarkable. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? Pes anserine bursitis<br><br>

I remember you mentioning at my last physical that weight loss would be good for me. And I think I might have overdone it. I&#x27;ve been doing a lot of pivoting and twisting on my knee while playing. **Doctor:** I see. Do you have any other medical conditions I should be aware of? **Patient:** Yes, I have polycystic ovarian syndrome and I&#x27;m currently taking oral contraceptive pills for that. **Doctor:** Alright. Let&#x27;s take a look. I&#x27;m going to check your vitals first. Now, let&#x27;s examine your knee. **(Doctor examines the patient&#x27;s knee)** **Doctor:** Okay, I&#x27;m feeling some tenderness on the inside of your lower leg, just below your kneecap on the tibia. And I see here that your BMI is 37. Your exam is also notable for some facial hair, which is consistent with your PCOS. The rest of your lower extremity exam seems normal. Based on everything you&#x27;ve told me and what I&#x27;ve found during the examination, I believe the most likely diagnosis is **Pes anserine bursitis**.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A group of investigators conducted a randomized controlled trial to compare the effectiveness of rivaroxaban to warfarin for ischemic stroke prevention in patients with atrial fibrillation. A total of 14,000 participants were enrolled and one half was assigned to each of the cohorts. The patients were followed prospectively for 3 years. At the conclusion of the trial, the incidence of ischemic stroke in participants taking rivaroxaban was 1.7% compared to 2.2% in participants taking warfarin. The hazard ratio is calculated as 0.79 and the 95% confidence interval is reported as 0.64 to 0.97. If the study was conducted with a total of 7,000 participants, which of the following changes would most be expected? Increased confidence interval range<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi [Patient Name], thanks for coming in today. I wanted to discuss a study I was reading about that&#x27;s relevant to atrial fibrillation and stroke prevention. **Patient:** Okay, I&#x27;m listening. **Doctor:** So, some researchers did a study comparing rivaroxaban, which you&#x27;re familiar with, to warfarin for preventing strokes in people with A-fib. They enrolled 14,000 patients, splitting them evenly into two groups. One group received rivaroxaban, and the other received warfarin. They followed everyone for three years. **Patient:** And what did they find? **Doctor:** Well, after those three years, they found that 1.7% of the people on rivaroxaban had an ischemic stroke, compared to 2.2% of the people on warfarin. They calculated something called a hazard ratio, which was 0.79, and the 95% confidence interval was 0.64 to 0.97. **Patient:** That sounds... complicated. What does it all *mean*? **Doctor:** Basically, it suggests that rivaroxaban might be slightly better at preventing strokes than warfarin in this group. Now, imagine they did the exact same study, same patients, same follow-up, but they only had 7,000 participants instead of 14,000. What do you think would happen to the certainty of their results? **Patient:** Hmm... I guess they would be less certain? **Doctor:** Exactly. The most likely change would be: **Increased confidence interval range**.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 34-year-old man comes to the physician because of fatigue and shortness of breath with moderate exertion for the past 2 months. Over the past 10 days, he has had low-grade fevers and night sweats. He has no history of serious illness except for a bicuspid aortic valve diagnosed 5 years ago. He has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 10 years and drinks 35 beers on social occasions. He does not use illicit drugs. The patient takes no medications. He appears weak. His temperature is 37.7C (99.9F), pulse is 70/min, and blood pressure is 128/64 mm Hg. The lungs are clear to auscultation. A grade 2/6 systolic murmur is heard best at the right sternal border and second intercostal space. There are several hemorrhages underneath his fingernails on both hands and multiple tender, red nodules on his fingers. Which of the following is the most likely causal organism? Streptococcus sanguinis<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, please have a seat. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Well, Doctor, for the past couple of months I&#x27;ve been feeling really tired and short of breath, even when I&#x27;m not doing much. **Doctor:** I see. And has anything else been going on? **Patient:** Yeah, for the last ten days or so, I&#x27;ve had these low-grade fevers and night sweats. **Doctor:** Okay. Do you have any past medical history I should be aware of? **Patient:** I was diagnosed with a bicuspid aortic valve about five years ago, but other than that, nothing serious. **Doctor:** Alright. And what about lifestyle habits? Do you smoke, drink, or use any drugs? **Patient:** I smoke about a pack of cigarettes a day, and I drink 3 to 5 beers when I&#x27;m out socializing. No drugs, though. **Doctor:** Are you currently taking any medications? **Patient:** No, nothing at all. **Doctor:** Okay. Let&#x27;s take a look at you. You do seem a little weak. Your temperature is 99.9, your pulse is 70, and your blood pressure is 128 over 64. Your lungs sound clear. I do hear a murmur, a grade 2/6 systolic murmur, loudest at the right sternal border and second intercostal space. Hmm, and I also see some hemorrhages under your fingernails and these tender, red nodules on your fingers. Based on everything, including your bicuspid valve, the murmur, the fever, and those findings on your hands, the most likely causal organism is **Streptococcus sanguinis**.

4 - Fully Covered: All essential information is present and clearly conveyed.
3 - Mostly Covered: Most key ideas are included, with minor omissions
2 - Moderately Covered: About half of the vital information is included.
1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.
0 - Not Covered: The dialogue misses or distorts the core information.

MC145. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 24-year-old man presents to the emergency department after a motor vehicle collision. He was the front seat and unrestrained driver in a head on collision. His temperature is 99.2F (37.3C), blood pressure is 90/65 mmHg, pulse is 152/min, respirations are 16/min, and oxygen saturation is 100% on room air. Physical exam is notable for a young man who opens his eyes spontaneously and is looking around. He answers questions with inappropriate responses but discernible words. He withdraws from pain but does not have purposeful movement. Which of the following is this patients Glasgow coma scale? 11<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, I&#x27;m Dr. Ramirez. What happened? **Patient:** (Groans) Car... headache... **Doctor:** You were in a car accident, correct? We&#x27;re in the emergency department now. Do you remember anything about the accident? **Patient:** (Incoherent mumbling) Front... **Doctor:** Okay. We&#x27;re checking you out. Your temperature is 99.2, your blood pressure is a little low at 90 over 65, and your heart rate is quite fast at 152. You&#x27;re breathing okay, 16 breaths per minute, and your oxygen level is perfect on its own. **Doctor:** Can you open your eyes for me? Good, you&#x27;re doing that spontaneously. Can you tell me where you are? **Patient:** (Confused) Blue... table... **Doctor:** You&#x27;re answering my questions, but not really making sense. Can you move your arm for me if I pinch it? **Patient:** (Withdraws arm from pain) **Doctor:** Okay. We&#x27;re just assessing your level of consciousness. Based on what I&#x27;ve seen so far, your Glasgow Coma Scale score is **11**.

1 - Minimally Covered: Only a few important points are mentioned.

MC146. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 34-year-old Caucasian female presents at the ER with fever and sharp pain in her chest upon coughing and inhalation. Three weeks earlier she presented to her rheumatologist with a butterfly rash, joint pain and fatigue and was given a diagnosis of systemic lupus erythematosus. A friction rub is present upon physical exam. Which of the following do you most suspect in this patient? Pericarditis<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, good morning. I see you&#x27;re 34 years old. What brings you to the ER today? **Patient:** Good morning, Doctor. I&#x27;ve been having a fever and this really sharp pain in my chest. It gets much worse when I cough or even just take a deep breath. **Doctor:** Okay, I understand. And have you been experiencing any other symptoms recently? **Patient:** Well, its kind of related, I think. About three weeks ago, I saw my rheumatologist because I had this butterfly rash on my face, and my joints were really hurting, and I was just so tired all the time. **Doctor:** And what did your rheumatologist say? **Patient:** She diagnosed me with systemic lupus erythematosus. **Doctor:** Okay, I see. Let me listen to your chest for a moment. (Doctor examines patient) Hmm, I can hear a friction rub. Based on everything you&#x27;ve told me, including your recent lupus diagnosis and the symptoms you&#x27;re experiencing now, I most suspect **Pericarditis**.

<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 47-year-old man with a history of alcoholism undergoes an upper endoscopy, which reveals a superficial mucosal tear in the distal esophagus. Laboratory results show a metabolic alkalosis. What is the most likely mechanism of the acid/base disturbance in this patient? Vomiting<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, Mr. Jones. Thanks for coming in. I understand you&#x27;ve been having some issues? **Mr. Jones:** Yes, Doctor. I havent been feeling well at all. **Doctor:** Okay. To help me understand, we performed an upper endoscopy. The results showed a small tear in the lining of your esophagus, down near the stomach. **Mr. Jones:** A tear? Is that serious? **Doctor:** It&#x27;s a superficial tear. Also, your lab results came back, and they show you have something called metabolic alkalosis. **Mr. Jones:** Metabolic what? What does that mean? **Doctor:** It means your body&#x27;s acid-base balance is off. Now, you mentioned you have a history of alcoholism, correct? **Mr. Jones:** Yes, that&#x27;s right. **Doctor:** Given everything  the tear in your esophagus, the metabolic alkalosis, and your history  the most likely reason for this acid-base imbalance is... Vomiting

MC148. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 60-year-old man comes to the physician because of flank pain, rash, and blood-tinged urine for 1 day. Two months ago, he was started on hydrochlorothiazide for hypertension. He takes acetaminophen for back pain. Examination shows a generalized, diffuse maculopapular rash. Serum studies show a creatinine concentration of 3.0 mg/dL. Renal ultrasonography shows no abnormalities. Which of the following findings is most likely to be observed in this patient? Urinary eosinophils<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Good morning, please have a seat. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Well, I&#x27;ve been having some flank pain, and I noticed a rash has popped up all over my body. Also, my urine looks a little bloody. This has all been happening for about a day now. **Doctor:** I see. And what medications are you currently taking? **Patient:** I started hydrochlorothiazide for my high blood pressure about two months ago. I also take acetaminophen occasionally for back pain. **Doctor:** Okay. Let&#x27;s take a look at that rash. *[Doctor examines the patient]* It looks like a generalized, diffuse maculopapular rash. I&#x27;m going to order some blood work and a renal ultrasound. **Patient:** Alright, doctor. *[Later, after reviewing the results]* **Doctor:** Okay, Mr. [Patient&#x27;s last name]. Your creatinine is elevated at 3.0 mg/dL, which suggests some kidney involvement. The renal ultrasound came back normal, which is good. Given your symptoms, the rash, the new medication, and the kidney issue, I&#x27;m suspecting a certain reaction. To confirm this, the most likely finding would be **Urinary eosinophils**.

MC149. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
Nucleic acid amplification testing (NAAT) of first-void urine confirms infection with Chlamydia trachomatis. Treatment with the appropriate pharmacotherapy is started. Which of the following health maintenance recommendations is most appropriate at this time? Avoid sun exposure<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi [Patient Name], thanks for coming in today. I&#x27;ve got your test results back. **Patient:** Okay, what did they say? **Doctor:** Well, the nucleic acid amplification test, or NAAT, that we did on your first-void urine sample came back positive. It confirms that you have an infection with Chlamydia trachomatis. **Patient:** Oh, okay. What does that mean for me? **Doctor:** It means we&#x27;ll start you on some medication to treat the infection. We&#x27;ll get that prescription sent to your pharmacy right away. **Patient:** Alright, that sounds good. Is there anything else I need to know or do? **Doctor:** Yes, actually. In addition to the medication, the most important thing I can recommend right now is to Avoid sun exposure.

MC150. To what extent do you think the dialogue captures all the essential information from the paragraph?<br><br>
<strong>Paragraph:</strong><br>
A 14-year-old boy presents as a new patient to your practice. While conducting your physical exam, you observe the findings depicted in Figures A and B. Which of the following additional findings would most likely be found in this patient? Iris hamartomas<br><br>
<strong>Dialogue:</strong><br>
>>Doctor:** Hi, welcome to the practice. I&#x27;m Dr. Smith. What brings you in today? **Patient:** Hi Dr. Smith. Im just here for a check-up, I guess. I&#x27;m a new patient. **Doctor:** Great. Well, let&#x27;s get started with a general physical exam. ( *Doctor begins the examination*) Okay, everything seems pretty normal so far. ( *Doctor examines the eyes*) Hmm, I&#x27;m noticing something interesting in your eyes, like what I see in these pictures (referring to Figures A and B). **Patient:** Interesting? What do you mean? Is something wrong? **Doctor:** Well, based on what I&#x27;m seeing in your eyes and comparing it to these images, I&#x27;d say that if we were to look for other related findings, the most likely thing we&#x27;d find would be **Iris hamartomas**.
